\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{k},i,\downarrow}=\begin{bmatrix}c_{\mathbf{k},Co_{1},d_{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}&c_{\mathbf{k},Co_{2},d_{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}&f_{\mathbf{k},Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\downarrow}&f_{\mathbf{k},Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}\end{bmatrix}_{i}^{T}

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k},ij,\sigma}=\begin{bmatrix}h_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{{}^{\prime}c}&h^{cf}_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}\\
h^{fc,{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}&h^{f}_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}\end{bmatrix}

(S12.157)

The band structures are shown in Fig.
S25
(b), where we can observe the formation of nodal lines at
k_{z}=1/2
plane. Without
C_{4z}
symmetry breaking, the system also develops a Dirac node along
\Gamma-Z
line. To simplify the analysis of the surface states, we consider a non-zero
C_{4z}
symmetry-breaking term to gap out this Dirac node.

XII.3
Surface states

We now discuss the surface states of the
fd
model we introduced in
Eq.

S12.157
. For given
k_{x},k_{y}
points, we can treat the system as an effective 1D system with the hopping matrix

\displaystyle h_{k_{z},i\sigma,j\sigma^{\prime}}^{1D,(k_{x},k_{y})}=h_{\mathbf{k},i\sigma,j\sigma^{\prime}}

(S12.158)

We now calculate the quantities we introduced in
Eq.

S12.148
.
For the
k_{x},k_{y}
outside the nodal line (near
M
point), we have
C=0
(
N_{+}=1,N_{-}=1
).
The ground state of the effective 1D system is adiabatically connected to the atomic insulators where all the
c
orbitals are filled. Therefore, we do not expect any non-trivial surface state for the momentum points outside the nodal line.

Inside the nodal line, we have
C=-2
with
N_{+}=0
, and
N_{-}=2
.
Since
N_{-}=2
, the filled bands can be decomposed into

\displaystyle\text{filled bands }\sim EBR_{-}\oplus EBR_{-}

(S12.159)

However, the EBR induced by
\psi_{c,\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}
electrons are

\displaystyle\psi_{c,\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}\sim EBR_{-}\oplus EBR_{+}

(S12.160)

The EBR induced by
\psi_{f,\mathbf{k}}
electrons are (see Tab.
S4
)

\displaystyle\psi_{f,\mathbf{k}}\sim EBR_{-}\oplus EBR_{+}

(S12.161)

Therefore, one of the
EBR_{-}
formed by the filled bands must come from the
f
orbitals.
f
-orbital is located at non-maximal Wyckoff positions
4b
and
EBR_{-}
corresponds to the Wannier states at maximal Wayckoff positions
2a
. Therefore, we could conclude that
f
orbitals develop molecular orbitals located at positions distinct from the Ce atoms.

The formation of the molecular orbitals for the
k_{x},k_{y}
inside the nodal line could then introduce surface states.
To gain a better understanding of the surface state, we investigate the low-energy effective Hamiltonian of the 1D system. The relevant low-energy degrees of freedom (electrons that appear near Fermi energy) are
\gamma_{c,\mathbf{k},4,\uparrow},\gamma_{c,\mathbf{k},2,\downarrow}
(solid blue lines in Fig.
S25
(a)), and
\gamma_{f,\mathbf{k},2,\sigma}
(solid red lines in Fig.
S25
(a)).
Transforming to the real space, we find

\displaystyle\gamma_{c,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),4,\uparrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Co_{3},d_{x^{2}-y^{2}},\uparrow}-c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Co_{4},d_{x^{2}-y^{2}},\uparrow}\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\uparrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\uparrow}+f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\uparrow}\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{c,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\downarrow}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Co_{1},d_{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}-c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Co_{2},d_{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\downarrow}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\downarrow}+f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}-1),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}\right)

(S12.162)

For the effective 1D system,
\gamma^{\dagger}_{c,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),4,\uparrow}
creates an electron at
R_{z}=3/4
, since it is formed by the
d
orbitals of Co
3
,Co
4
.
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\uparrow}
creates an electron at
1/4
since it corresponds to the bonding state of
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\uparrow}

and
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\uparrow}
.

\gamma^{\dagger}_{c,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\uparrow}
creates an electron at
R_{z}=1/4
, since it is formed by the
d
orbitals of Co
1
,Co
2
.
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z})2,\downarrow}
creates an electron at
3/4
since it corresponds to the bonding state of
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\downarrow}

and
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}-1),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}
.
The Wannier center of the electron operator can also be obtained by calculating the corresponding Berry phase. We can already observe that
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\sigma}
denotes the molecular orbital formed by
f
electrons.
Furthermore, we show the band structures and also the orbital weights for the effective 1D system in
Fig.

S26
.
We find that, outside the nodal line,
f
orbitals are fully empty and thus are irrelevant. This indicates the absence of the surface states.

Figure S26:
Dispersions of the effective 1D model in
Eq.

S12.157
at
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
(inside the nodal line) and
k_{x}=k_{y}=1/2
(outside the nodal line). Each band is two-fold degenerate due to
P\cdot\mathcal{T}
symmetry. Red and blue mark the orbital weights of
f
and
c
electrons respectively.
+,-
label the
\{M_{z}|\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\}
eigenvalues of the bands at
k_{z}=1/2
.

Figure S27:
Position of the electron orbitals for the effective 1D system for spin
\uparrow
and spin
\downarrow
sector.
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\uparrow/dn}
is located at
n\pm 1/4
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(big red dots).
\gamma_{c,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),4,\uparrow}
(
\gamma_{c,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\downarrow}
) is located at
n+3/4
(
n+1/4
), marked by blue dots in the upper (lower) axis. However, from
Eq.

S12.162
,
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\sigma}
is a molecular orbital.
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\uparrow}
is formed by the superposition of
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\uparrow}
at
n+0
and
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\uparrow}
at
n+1/2
(small red dots in the upper axis).
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\downarrow}
is formed by the superposition of
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}-1),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}
at
n-1/2
and
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\downarrow}
at
n
(small red dots in the lower axis). The solid line represents the slab geometry along
z
direction, where green denotes the first type of boundary (Boundary I, left) and orange denotes the second type of boundary (Boundary II, right).

We now discuss the surface states. Analyzing the 1D band structures shown in
Fig.

S26
(left panel), we observe a band inversion between the
f
-molecular orbital and the
c
orbital. This inversion suggests that the occupied band exhibits orbital contributions from both
f
and
c
orbitals. Consequently, a surface state could potentially emerge if the boundary cut through either the molecular
f
orbital or the bond formed between the
f
and
c
orbitals.

We next consider the open boundaries as illustrated in
Fig.

S27
, where the real-space positions of the relevant orbitals are also marked. Generally, there are two types of open boundaries, each distinguished by different colors in
Fig.

S27
. Both types of boundaries cut through the molecular orbitals (
\gamma_{f,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),2,\uparrow}
) of
f
electrons in the spin
\uparrow
sector. For the spin
\downarrow
sector, both boundary types slice through the bonds linking the
f
and
c
orbitals.

Furthermore, we could consider a boundary that cuts through the molecular
f
orbitals in the spin
\downarrow
sector and the
f
-
c
bonds in the spin
\uparrow
sector. However, these boundary configurations can be derived by applying a
P\cdot\mathcal{T}
transformation to our current boundaries, rendering them equivalent.

Figure S28:
Surface bands calculated via Hamiltonian given by
Eq.

S12.157
with open boundary along
z
directions. Two types of boundaries are illustrated in Fig.
S27
by solid and dashed lines. The color denotes the weights of the bands on the surfaces. We can observe two surface states inside the nodal lines.

Figure S29:
Surface bands with open boundary along
z
directions. Additional boundary terms defined in
Eqs.

S12.163
,
S12.164
,
S12.165
and
S12.166
have been introduced with
m_{1}=1.5,m_{2}=1.3,m_{3}=-1.0,m_{4}=0.4
. We can observe the surface states merge into the bulk spectrum.

In conclusion, we expect two surface states to emerge (each spin sector gives one surface state). This expectation has been confirmed by numerical calculations (
Fig.

S28
) using the Hamiltonian given by
Eq.

S12.157
.
To confirm our previous analysis, we now show that we can merge the surface states to the bulk spectrum by adding additional boundary terms.

We first consider the spin
\uparrow
sector. Boundary I (green boundary in
Fig.

S27
) cuts through the
f
molecular orbitals, and then we have an
f
operator located at the boundary

f_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\uparrow}
.
This
f
operator could still hybridize with
\gamma_{c,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}=0),4,\uparrow}
operator nearby which leads to a surface state. Therefore, we can consider the following boundary term

\displaystyle m_{1}(f_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\uparrow}^{\dagger}f_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\uparrow}+c_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Co_{4},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\uparrow}^{\dagger}c_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Co_{4},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\uparrow})

(S12.163)

to shift the energy of the corresponding surface states.

Boundary II (orange boundary
Fig.

S27
) cuts through the
f
molecular molecular in the spin
\uparrow
sector, and then we have an
f
operator located at the boundary
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\uparrow}

(where
R_{n}
denotes the corresponding unit cell at the boundary). It will hybridize with the nearby
c
electron
c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}-1),Co_{4},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\uparrow}
and creates a surface state.
We can consider the following boundary term

\displaystyle m_{2}(c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}-1),Co_{4},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\uparrow}^{\dagger}c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}-1),Co_{4},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\uparrow}+f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\uparrow}^{\dagger}f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}),Ce_{1},f_{xyz},\uparrow})

(S12.164)

to shift the energy of the corresponding surface states.

We next consider the spin
\downarrow
sector. Boundary I cuts through the bonds formed by
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},-1),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}
and
c_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Co_{2},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}
. Then we have a
c_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Co_{2},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}
operator located at the boundary which gives a surface state.
We can consider the following boundary term

\displaystyle m_{3}(c_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Co_{2},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}^{\dagger}c_{(k_{x},k_{y},0),Co_{2},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow})

(S12.165)

to shift the energy of the corresponding surface states.

Boundary II cuts through the bonds formed by
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}
and
c_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}+1),Co_{2},d{x^{2}-y^{2}},\downarrow}
in the spin
\downarrow
sector. Then we have
f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}
operator located at the boundary which gives a surface state.
We can consider the following boundary term

\displaystyle m_{4}(f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow}^{\dagger}f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{n}),Ce_{2},f_{xyz},\downarrow})

(S12.166)

to shift the energy of the corresponding surface states.

In
Fig.

S29
, we show the surface states with boundary terms defined in
Eqs.

S12.163
,
S12.164
,
S12.165
and
S12.166
. We can observe the additional boundary terms have shifted the energy of the surface states and the surface states have merged into the bulk spectrum.

Finally, we discuss the surface states of the realistic model obtained from the mean-field calculations (
Section

X.3
).
For the realistic model, there are more than one
d
orbitals per Co, more than one
f
orbitals per Ce and we also have
p
orbitals from P atoms. Therefore, we cannot use
N_{+},N_{-}
to conclude whether
f
forms obstructed orbitals or not. We take an open boundary along
z
directions and plot the surface bands in
Fig.

S30
. We observe multiple surface states emerge. Here, we provide several possible origins of the surface states.

Figure S30:
Surface states calculation with open boundary along
z
directions. Bands marked by dark red and dark blue denote the bands with large weights on the surface. The upper and lower panels represent the same surface spectrum, each displayed within distinct energy windows. In the bottom figures, we can observe several narrow surface bands that are related to the narrow bandwidth of the
f
electrons.

Figure S31:
Band structures of the Kondo phase obtained from mean-field calculations (
Section

X.3
). (a) Red and blue denote the orbital weights of two molecular orbitals of
f
electrons(
Eq.

S12.167
). (b) Red and blue denote the orbital weights of
f
and
c
electrons respectively, where we can observe a band inversion happens along
X
-
R
.

From the previous simple model, we observe that there are two mechanisms that can generate surface states

•

f
orbitals form molecular orbitals.

•

Band inversion between
f
electrons and
c
electrons.

These two mechanisms may also produce surface states in the realistic model. However, the details of the surface states are different since the simple model does not include all the orbitals.

We first show the obstructed nature of
f
bands in the realistic model. We introduce the following molecular orbitals (for the effective 1D system) in a similar manner in
Eq.

S12.162

\displaystyle\gamma_{f,\pm,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\uparrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mp s_{\alpha}f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},\alpha,\uparrow}+f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{2},\alpha,\uparrow}\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{f,\pm,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\downarrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\pm s_{\alpha}f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),Ce_{1},\alpha,\downarrow}+f_{(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}-1),Ce_{2},\alpha,\downarrow}\right)

(S12.167)

where

\displaystyle\alpha=f_{z^{3}},f_{xz^{2}},f_{yz^{2}},f_{z(x^{2}-y^{2})},f_{xyz},f_{x(x^{2}-3y^{2})},f_{y(3x^{2}-y^{2})}

\displaystyle s_{f_{z^{3}},f_{xz^{2}},f_{yz^{2}},f_{z(x^{2}-y^{2})},f_{xyz},f_{x(x^{2}-3y^{2})},f_{y(3x^{2}-y^{2})}}=-1,1,1,-1,-1,1,1

(S12.168)

For the effective 1D system
\gamma_{f,\pm,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
is equivalent to an
s
-orbital with spin
\mp\sigma
at
R_{z}=1/4
, and
\gamma_{f,\pm,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
is equivalent to an
s
-orbital with spin
\pm\sigma
at
R_{z}=3/4
.
If there is a large enough energy splitting between
\gamma_{f,+,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
and
\gamma_{f,-,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
states, we could conclude there is a formation of molecular orbitals.

In
Fig.

S31
(a), we plot the orbital weights of
\gamma_{f,+,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
and
\gamma_{f,-,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
.
We can observe the splitting between
\gamma_{f,+,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
bands and
\gamma_{f,-,\alpha,(k_{x},k_{y},R_{z}),\sigma}
bands near
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
, which indicates the
f
-electrons tend to form molecular orbitals in the ground state. However, the corresponding surface states may merge into the bulk spectrum.

Secondly, as we show in
Fig.

S31
(b), a band inversion between
f
and
c
electrons also appears in the realistic model. However, in the realistic model, the band inversion appears at
k_{x}=0,k_{y}=1/2
(
X
-
R
), but is absent at
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
. This is because our simple model overestimates the
z
-direction hopping of
d_{x^{2}-y^{2}}
orbitals which creates the band inversion at
k_{x}=k_{y}=0
in the simple model, and has ignored the
d_{xz},d_{yz}
orbitals which creates the band inversion at
k_{x}=0,k_{y}=1/2
in the realistic model.

Finally, there are also strong bonds formed by
p_{z}
orbitals of P atoms (as we have also discussed in
Appendix

VII
, see also
Fig.

S14
). Cutting
p_{z}
bonds could also create surface states.

In conclusion, our study demonstrates that various mechanisms can create surface states in this system. However, it is important to note that the existence of these surface states is not protected by symmetries, allowing them to potentially merge into the bulk spectrum.
Moreover, it is worth mentioning that, due to the narrow bandwidth and correlation effects of
f
-electron systems, DFT calculations may not accurately capture all the details of the band structures.
Despite the complexity of real materials, our research outlines several potential mechanisms for the generation of surface states observed in experiments, and shows there is not a topological reason for their appearances.

Appendix XIII
Schrieffer-Wolff transformation

In this section, we discuss the Schrieffer-Wolff (SW) transformation
[
78
,
76
]
of generic many-body systems.
In Sec.
IX
, we use the Schrieffer-Wolff transformation to obtain the Kondo lattice model from the periodic Anderson model. In Sec.
XIV
, we also use the Schrieffer-Wolff transformation to demonstrate the flat bands could induce a type-A antiferromagnetic order.

We consider an unperturbed Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{0}=\sum_{L,i}E_{L,i}|L,i\rangle\langle L,i|+\sum_{H,i}E_{H,i}|H,i\rangle\langle H,i|

(S13.169)

where
|L,i\rangle
and
|H,i\rangle
denote the eigenstate in the low-energy and high-energy subspaces. These eigenvalues and eigenvectors are obtained by directly diagonalizing
H_{0}
. We then introduce the following projection operators

\displaystyle P_{L}=\sum_{i}|L,i\rangle\langle L,i|,\quad P_{H}=\sum_{i}|H,i\rangle\langle H,i|

(S13.170)

We then introduce the perturbation term

\displaystyle H_{1}=\sum_{ij}V_{ij}|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|+\text{h.c.}

(S13.171)

The perturbation term maps a low-energy state to a high-energy state or a high-energy state to a low-energy state. We then find

\displaystyle P_{L}H_{1}P_{H}=\sum_{ij}V_{ij}|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|

\displaystyle P_{H}H_{1}P_{L}=\sum_{ij}V^{*}_{ij}|H,j\rangle\langle L,i|

(S13.172)

We next aim to obtain an effective Hamiltonian defined in the low-energy subspace that takes into account the effects of the high-energy subspace in perturbation theory.

We consider the following operators

\displaystyle S=\sum_{i,j}V_{ij}\frac{|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}-\sum_{i,j}V^{*}_{ij}\frac{|H,j\rangle\langle L,i|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}

(S13.173)

We perform a unitary transformation correspondingly

\displaystyle H^{\prime}=e^{S}(H_{0}+H_{1})e^{-S}=H_{0}+H_{1}+[S,H_{0}]+[S,H_{1}]+\frac{1}{2}[S,[S,H_{0}]]+....

(S13.174)

We note that

\displaystyle[S,H_{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}V_{ij}E_{H,j}\frac{|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|H,j\rangle\langle H,j|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}-\sum_{i,j}V_{ij}E_{L,i}\frac{|L,i\rangle\langle L,i|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j}V^{*}_{ij}E_{L,i}\frac{|H,j\rangle\langle L,i|L,i\rangle\langle L,i|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}+\sum_{i,j}V^{*}_{ij}E_{H,j}\frac{|H,j\rangle\langle H,j|H,j\rangle\langle L,i|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}-V_{ij}|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|-\sum_{i,j}-V_{ij}^{*}|H,j\rangle\langle L,i|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-H_{1}

(S13.175)

Therefore, the new effective Hamiltonian now becomes

\displaystyle H^{\prime}\approx H_{0}+H_{1}-H_{1}+[S,H_{1}]-\frac{1}{2}[S,H_{1}]=H_{0}+\frac{1}{2}[S,H_{1}]

(S13.176)

We note that

\displaystyle[S,H_{1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,i^{\prime},j}V_{ij}\frac{|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}V_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}|H,j\rangle\langle L,i^{\prime}|-\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}}V_{ij}^{*}\frac{|H,j\rangle\langle L,i|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}V_{ij^{\prime}}|L,i\rangle\langle H,j^{\prime}|

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,i^{\prime},j}V_{ij}|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|(-V^{*}_{i^{\prime}j})\frac{|H,j\rangle\langle L,i^{\prime}|}{E_{L,i^{\prime}}-E_{H,j}}-\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}}V_{ij}^{*}|H,j\rangle\langle L,i|V_{ij^{\prime}}\frac{|L,i\rangle\langle H,j^{\prime}|}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,i^{\prime},j}V_{ij}V_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}\bigg(\frac{1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}+\frac{1}{E_{L,i^{\prime}}-E_{H,j}}\bigg)|L,i\rangle\langle L,i^{\prime}|+\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}}V_{ij}^{*}V_{ij^{\prime}}\bigg(\frac{-1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}+\frac{-1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j^{\prime}}}\bigg)|H,j\rangle\langle H,j^{\prime}|

(S13.177)

Using Eq.
S13.174
, we obtain

\displaystyle H^{\prime}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sum_{L,i}E_{L,i}|L,i\rangle\langle L,i|+\sum_{H,i}E_{H,i}|H,i\rangle\langle H,i|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,i^{\prime},j}V_{ij}V_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}\bigg(\frac{1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}+\frac{1}{E_{L,i^{\prime}}-E_{H,j}}\bigg)|L,i\rangle\langle L,i^{\prime}|+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}}V_{ij}^{*}V_{ij^{\prime}}\bigg(\frac{-1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j}}+\frac{-1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,j^{\prime}}}\bigg)|H,j\rangle\langle H,j^{\prime}|

(S13.178)

Finally, we only keep the term in the low-energy subspace, which gives

\displaystyle H_{eff}

\displaystyle=P_{L}H^{\prime}P_{L}=H_{0}^{\prime}+H_{SW}

\displaystyle H_{0}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle P_{L}H_{0}P_{L}=\sum_{i}E_{L,i}|L,i\rangle\langle L,i|

\displaystyle H_{SW}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j,m}V_{im}V_{jm}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,m}}+\frac{1}{E_{L,j}-E_{H,m}}\right)|L,i\rangle\langle L,j|

(S13.179)

H_{0}^{\prime}
denotes the contribution from the original unperturbed Hamiltonian and
H_{SW}
denotes the additional contribution generated from the Schrieffer-Wolff transformation. In practice, the additional term
H_{SW}
describes the following virtual process

\displaystyle|L,j\rangle\xrightarrow{V_{jm}^{*}|H,m\rangle\langle L,j|}|H,m\rangle\xrightarrow{V_{im}|L,i\rangle\langle H,m|}|L,i\rangle

(S13.180)

where

H_{1}
maps a low-energy state
|L,j\rangle
to a high-energy state
|H,m\rangle
and then map the high-energy state
|H,m\rangle
back to a low-energy state
|L,i\rangle

Appendix XIV
Flat-band ferromagnetism/anti-ferromagnetism

In this section, we demonstrate that a relatively flat band that is atomic along
z
direction and non-atomic along
x,y
directions leads to a type-A antiferromagnetism (in-plane magnetism and out-of-plane antiferromagnetism).

Several comments are in order:

•

In this section, we will pick a toy model to demonstrate the ferromagnetism/antiferromagnetism tendency induced by flat bands.

•

We will consider the case of a half-filled single flat band to simplify the considerations.

•

We will ignore the contributions of the other dispersive bands. The flat band, when it appears near Fermi energy, induces a large density of states. We expect the leading-order instability to be generated by flat bands instead of dispersive bands.

•

We comment that the real materials are more complicated, in which the flat bands are not perfectly flat and the dispersive bands could also make contributions. However, based on the toy model we considered in this appendix and the more realistic DFT model we discussed in Appendix
VI
, we could conclude that flat bands (atomic along
z
direction and non-atomic along
xy
direction) indeed favor the type-A antiferromagnetism.

In Sec.
XIV.1
, we will show that a 2D flat band which is non-atomic along
xy
directions leads to an in-plane ferromagnetism. In Sec.
XIV.2
, we show that, a weak hopping along
z
directions leads to an antiferromagnetic coupling along
z
direction and stabilizes a type-A antiferromagnetism.

XIV.1
Ferromagnetism from 2D non-atomic flat band

In this section, we provide more understanding of the ferromagnetism induced by flat bands, based on a single-flat-band toy model. We use
d_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}
to denote the electron operators with momentum
\mathbf{k}
, orbital
\alpha
and spin
\sigma
.

We assume a flat band (with two-fold degeneracy due to
SU(2)
spin symmetry) can be described by the following electron operators in the band basis

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}=\sum_{\alpha}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})d_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}

(S14.181)

V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
describes the wavefunction of the flat band. We assume
V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
is
k_{z}
-independent since the flat band is atomic along
z
direction.

We first consider perfect flat bands with the kinetic term in the band basis taken to be

\displaystyle H_{0}=\sum_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}E_{0}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}

(S14.182)

To simplify the calculations, we consider the case where the flat band is half-filled at the non-interacting limit with
E_{0}=0
.
We next consider the interaction term. We take the following density-density interactions characterized by interaction matrix
U_{\alpha\gamma}
with
U_{\alpha\gamma}=U_{\gamma\alpha}>0
(we also drop the Hund’s coupling term here, since it is usually small compared to the density-density interactions)

\displaystyle H_{U}=\sum_{\alpha,\mathbf{R},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\frac{U_{\alpha\gamma}}{2}\bigg(P:d_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}d_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}:P\bigg)\bigg(P:d_{\mathbf{R},\gamma,\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}d_{\mathbf{R},\gamma,\sigma^{\prime}}:P\bigg)

(S14.183)

where
P
is the projection operator that projects the electron operator to the band basis

\displaystyle Pd_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}P=V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}

(S14.184)

and
::
denotes normal ordering.
We consider the case of
E_{0}=0
. Then we have
\langle\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\gamma,\sigma}\rangle_{0}=\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}\delta_{\alpha,\gamma}
which corresponds to the half-filling of flat bands.
The projected density operators can then be written as

\displaystyle:Pd_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}d_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}P:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}}\frac{e^{+i\mathbf{q}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}}{N}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\alpha,\sigma}-\langle\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}}\frac{e^{+i\mathbf{q}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}}{N}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\alpha,\sigma}\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}}\frac{e^{+i\mathbf{q}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}}{N}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\bigg(\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\alpha,\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}\bigg)

(S14.185)

The interaction term can be written as

\displaystyle H_{U}=\sum_{\alpha,\gamma,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{q},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\frac{U_{\alpha\gamma}}{2N}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})(\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\sigma}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}}{2})(\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}}{2})

(S14.186)

The total Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle H_{0}+H_{U}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\gamma,\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{q},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\frac{U_{\alpha\gamma}}{2N}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})(\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\sigma}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}}{2})(\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}}{2})

(S14.187)

where we take
E_{0}=0
.

We now aim to rewrite the Hamiltonian in a more compact formula. We can perform an eigen-decomposition of the interaction matrix
U_{\alpha\gamma}

\displaystyle U_{\alpha\gamma}=\sum_{n=1,...,n_{orb}}u_{n}v_{\alpha n}v^{*}_{\gamma n}

(S14.188)

with
u_{n}
and
v_{\alpha n}
are eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
U_{\alpha\gamma}
matrix.
n_{orb}
denotes the number of orbitals. The Hamiltonian now becomes (using Eq.
S14.187
, and Eq.
S14.188
)

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle\sum_{n,\mathbf{q}}\frac{u_{n}}{2N}\bigg[\sum_{\mathbf{k},\sigma,\alpha}v_{\alpha n}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})(\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\sigma}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}}{2})\bigg]\bigg[\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\gamma}v^{*}_{\gamma n}V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})V^{*}_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q})(\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q},\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}}{2})\bigg]

(S14.189)

Therefore, we find

\displaystyle H=\sum_{n,q}\frac{u_{n}}{2}O_{n,\mathbf{q}}O_{n,\mathbf{q}}^{\dagger}

(S14.190)

with

\displaystyle O_{n,\mathbf{q}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\sigma,\alpha}v_{\alpha n}V^{*}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})(\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\sigma}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q},0}}{2})

(S14.191)

We now discuss the values of
v_{n}
which are the eigenvalues of
U_{\alpha\gamma}
. In general, we expect
v_{n}
to be positive. The negative
v_{n}
indicates an attractive interaction in certain channels. This could then lead to additional instability such as orbital ordering. To observe this, we take a two-orbital case as an example. The interaction matrix of a two-orbital model can be written as

\displaystyle U_{\alpha\gamma}=\begin{bmatrix}U_{1}&U_{2}\\
U_{2}&U_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\alpha\gamma}

(S14.192)

where
U_{1},U_{2}
denotes intra and inter-orbital interactions. Obviously, the eigenvalues of the interaction matrix are
U_{1}+U_{2},U_{1}-U_{2}
. If
U_{2}>U_{1}
, one of the eigenvalues becomes negative. To observe its consequence, we could write the interaction term via density operators as

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\bigg[\frac{U_{1}}{2}(\nu_{\mathbf{R},1}^{2}+\nu_{\mathbf{R},2}^{2})+U_{2}\nu_{\mathbf{R},1}\nu_{\mathbf{R},2}\bigg]=\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\bigg[\frac{U_{1}+U_{2}}{4}(\nu_{\mathbf{R},1}+\nu_{\mathbf{R},2})^{2}+\frac{U_{1}-U_{2}}{4}(\nu_{\mathbf{R},1}-\nu_{\mathbf{R},2})^{2}\bigg]

(S14.193)

with the density operator defined as

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=\sum_{\sigma}:d_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\sigma}^{\dagger}d_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\sigma}:

(S14.194)

We note that, if
U_{1}-U_{2}<0
, the system minimizes the energy by only filling one of the two orbitals (maximizing
|\nu_{\mathbf{R},1}-\nu_{\mathbf{R},2}|
), which could induce an orbital ordering.

However, for the generic system, the diagonal components of
U_{\alpha\gamma}
(which corresponds to the intra-orbital repulsion) are usually stronger than the off-diagonal components (which correspond to the inter-orbital repulsion). The strong enough diagonal components of
U_{\alpha\gamma}
matrix could make all the eigenvalues positive
u_{n}>0
. In the current case, since we are mostly interested in the formation of magnetism (instead of orbital ordering), we will assume
u_{n}\geq 0
for all
n
.

When
u_{n}>0
,

\displaystyle H=\sum_{n,q}\frac{u_{n}}{2}O_{n,\mathbf{q}}O_{n,\mathbf{q}}^{\dagger}

(S14.195)

becomes a positive semi-definite Hamiltonian. The state
|\phi\rangle
that satisfy

\displaystyle O_{n,\mathbf{q}}^{\dagger}|\phi\rangle=0,\quad\forall n,\mathbf{q}

(S14.196)

would be the ground state
[
127
]
.

Since we assume the system is an effective 2D system where
V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
is
k_{z}
independent,
we treat
xy
directions and
z
direction separately.
We use
\mathbf{k}^{xy},\mathbf{q}^{xy}
to denote the in-plane components of the vector with
\mathbf{k}^{xy}=(k_{x},k_{y}),\mathbf{q}^{xy}=(q_{x},q_{y})
. Therefore, we can rewrite
O_{\alpha,\mathbf{q}}^{\dagger}
as

\displaystyle O^{\dagger}_{n,\mathbf{q}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\alpha,\sigma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k}^{xy})\sum_{k_{z}}(\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},k_{z}+q^{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},k^{z}),\sigma}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},0}\delta_{q^{z},0}}{2})

(S14.197)

Since the system is atomic along
z
direction, we can perform Fourier transformation along
z
direction with

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{L_{z}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},k_{z}),\sigma}e^{ik_{z}R_{z}}

(S14.198)

where
L_{\alpha}
is the system size along
\alpha
direction with the total number of unit cells
N=L_{x}L_{y}L_{z}
. Then we find

\displaystyle O^{\dagger}_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R^{z})}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{L_{z}}}\sum_{q_{z}}O^{\dagger}_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},q^{z})}e^{-iq_{z}R_{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\alpha,\sigma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})(\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},0}}{2})

(S14.199)

where
N_{xy}=L_{x}L_{y}
.
The Hamiltonian now can be written as

\displaystyle H_{0}+H_{U}=\sum_{n,\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}}\frac{u_{n}}{2}O_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})}O_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})}^{\dagger}

(S14.200)

where we can observe that the Hamiltonian of each layer is decoupled.
We now solve the positive semidefinite Hamiltonian of each
R_{z}
layer

\displaystyle H_{R_{z}}=\sum_{n,\mathbf{q}^{xy}}\frac{u_{n}}{2}O_{\alpha,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R^{z})}O^{\dagger}_{\alpha,(\mathbf{q}^{zy},R_{z})}

(S14.201)

We now prove that the following states are the ground states of
H_{R_{z}}

\displaystyle|R_{z}\rangle=\prod_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

(S14.202)

where
|0\rangle
is the vacuum state. We prove it by showing

\displaystyle O^{\dagger}_{\alpha,(\mathbf{q}^{zy},R_{z})}|R_{z}\rangle=0

(S14.203)

Written explicitly, we have

\displaystyle O_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})}^{\dagger}|R_{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\alpha,\sigma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})(\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},0})\prod_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\alpha}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})(\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},0}}{2}-\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger})\prod_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\alpha,\sigma=\downarrow}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})(-\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},0}}{2})\prod_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(S14.204)

Therefore,
|R_{z}\rangle
is the ground states of
H_{R_{z}}
.
The ground state in Eq.
S14.202
defines a state where the spin
\uparrow
flavor of the flat band is fully filled. Due to the
SU(2)
rotation symmetry, all the other states obtained by acting
SU(2)
symmetry on the
|R_{z}\rangle
are also ground states. The states obtained by
SU(2)
transformation can be written as

\displaystyle|R_{z},\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})\rangle=\hat{R}_{R_{z}}(\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z}))|R_{z}\rangle

(S14.205)

where
\hat{R}_{R_{z}}(\theta,\phi)
describes the following
SU(2)
symmetry transformation

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{R_{z}}(\theta,\phi)\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}\hat{R}_{R_{z}}^{\dagger}(\theta,\phi)=\cos(\theta)e^{i\phi}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}+\sin(\theta)\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}

(S14.206)

Therefore a generic ground state of the whole system can be obtained by taking the tensor product of the ground state of each layer

\displaystyle|\theta,\phi\rangle=\bigotimes_{R_{z}}|R_{z},\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.207)

where
\theta,\phi
are functions of
R_{z}
that characterize the
SU(2)
rotation of each layer. Since the system is decoupled along
z
direction (in the case of zero
z
-direction hopping), we observe the relative spin directions of different layers can be arbitrary. In other words, there is no
z
direction magnetic order.

XIV.1.1
Orthonormal basis

The ground states of the Hamiltonian characterized by

\displaystyle|\theta,\phi\rangle=\bigotimes_{R_{z}}|R_{z},\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.208)

have a large degeneracy. Moreover, the set of ground states,

\{|\theta,\phi\rangle|\theta\in[0,\pi),\phi\in[0,2\pi)\}
, does not form an orthonormal basis of
the ground-state manifold.

For future convenience, we now aim to find the orthonormal basis of the ground-state manifold. For layer
R_{z}
, the ground state is characterized by

\displaystyle|R_{z},\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.209)

The Hamiltonian has a
SU(2)
symmetry for each layer. The generators of
SU(2)
group (or the spin operators) are

\displaystyle S^{x,y,z}_{R_{z}}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\frac{\sigma^{x,y,z}_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}}{2}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime}}

(S14.210)

where
\sigma^{x,y,z}
are Pauli matrices.
We find the ground states of layer
R_{z}
which are characterized by
\{|R_{z},\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})\rangle\}_{\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})}
form a spin
S=(N_{xy}/2)
representation of the
SU(2)
group, where
N_{xy}
denotes number of unit cells for each layer.
The spin
S=(N_{xy}/2)
representation corresponds to the ferromagnetic state where all the filled flat-band electrons have the same spin direction.
To show the ground states indeed form
S=(N_{xy}/2)
representation, we calculate

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}(S^{\mu}_{R_{z}})^{2}|R_{z},\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})\rangle=\sum_{\mu}(S^{\mu}_{R_{z}})^{2}\hat{R}_{R_{z}}(\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z}))|R_{z},0,0\rangle=\hat{R}_{R_{z}}(\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z}))\sum_{\mu}(S^{\mu}_{R_{z}})^{2}|R_{z},0,0\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S(S+1)\hat{R}_{R_{z}}(\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z}))|R_{z},0,0\rangle=S(S+1)|R_{z},\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.211)

where we use the fact that the Casimir operator
\sum_{\mu}(S^{\mu}_{R_{z}})^{2}
commutes with the
SU(2)
rotation operator
\hat{R}_{R_{z}}(\theta(R_{z}),\phi(R_{z}))
. From Eq.
S14.211
, we conclude the ground states form
S=(N_{xy}/2)
representation.

Since the degenerate ground states form an
S=(N_{xy}/2)
representation, we can then use the eigenstates of
S^{z}_{R_{z}}
operator and the ladder operators to build the orthonormal basis of the ground-state manifolds. We introduce the following orthonormal states to characterize the ground-state manifolds

\displaystyle\{|R_{z},M\rangle\}_{M=-S}^{M=S}

\displaystyle S_{R_{z}}^{z}|R_{z},M\rangle=M|R_{z},M\rangle,\quad\sum_{\mu}(S_{R_{z}}^{\mu})^{2}|R_{z},M\rangle=S(S+1)|R_{z},M\rangle

(S14.212)

The above orthonormal basis can be obtained by acting ladder operators on the
|R_{z},\theta(R_{z})=0,\phi(R_{z})=0\rangle
. In other words, we can let

\displaystyle|R_{z},M=S\rangle=|R_{z},\theta(R_{z})=0,\phi(R_{z})=0\rangle

\displaystyle|R_{z},M-1\rangle=\frac{S_{R_{z}}^{-}}{\sqrt{S(S+1)-M(M-1)}}|R_{z},M\rangle

(S14.213)

where
S_{R_{z}}^{-}=S_{R_{z}}^{+}-iS_{R_{z}}^{-}
is the spin ladder operator.

We now prove we can indeed construct
|R_{z},M\rangle
via Eq.
S14.213
.
We first prove that
|R_{z},\theta(R_{z})=0,\phi(R_{z})=0\rangle
is the eigenstate of
S_{R_{z}}^{z}
operator with eigenvalue
S
:

\displaystyle S_{R_{z}}^{z}|R_{z},\theta(R_{z})=0,\phi(R_{z})=0\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\sigma}\sigma\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}\prod_{\mathbf{q}^{xy}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle=\frac{N_{xy}}{2}\prod_{\mathbf{q}^{xy}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S|R_{z},\theta(R_{z})=0,\phi(R_{z})=0\rangle

(S14.214)

Therefore,
|R_{z},\theta(R_{z})=0,\phi(R_{z})=0\rangle=|R_{z},M=S\rangle
.

We next show that, for a given
|R_{z},M\rangle
, we can generate
|R_{z},M-1\rangle
by acting spin ladder operator (
S_{R_{z}}^{-}=S_{R_{z}}^{+}-iS_{R_{z}}^{-}
) on
|R_{z},M\rangle
. Via spin commutation relation, we find

\displaystyle S^{z}_{R_{z}}\bigg(\frac{S_{R_{z}}^{-}}{\sqrt{S(S+1)-M(M-1)}}|R_{z},M\rangle\bigg)=\bigg(S_{R_{z}}^{-}S_{R_{z}}^{z}-S_{R_{z}}^{-}\bigg)\frac{1}{\sqrt{S(S+1)-M(M-1)}}|R_{z},M\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{S_{R_{z}}^{-}}{\sqrt{S(S+1)-M(M-1)}}(M-1)|R_{z},M\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(M-1)\bigg(\frac{S_{R_{z}}^{-}}{\sqrt{S(S+1)-M(M-1)}}|R_{z},M\rangle\bigg)

(S14.215)

where the additional
\frac{1}{\sqrt{S(S+1)-M(M-1)}}
factor ensures the normalization condition
\langle R_{z},M|R_{z},M\rangle=1
. Therefore, we prove that, we can construct
|R_{z},M-1\rangle
state from
|R_{z},M\rangle
state via Eq.
S14.213
(second line).

We can also rewrite Eq.
S14.213
as following

\displaystyle|R_{z},M\rangle=\alpha_{S,M}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-M}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle\alpha_{S,S}=1

\displaystyle\alpha_{S,M}=\bigg(\prod_{i=M}^{S-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{S(S+1)-i(i+1)}}\bigg),\quad\text{ for }M<S

(S14.216)

where
\alpha_{S,M}
is the normalization factor to ensure
\langle R_{z},M|R_{z},M\rangle=1
.

Taking the tensor product of the states from different layers, we can introduce the following orthogonal basis for the ground-state manifold

\displaystyle\{|m\rangle\},\quad\quad|m\rangle=\bigotimes_{R_{z}}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.217)

where we have defined
m
as a function of
R_{z}
with
m(R_{z})
characterizes the states of layer
R_{z}
.

XIV.1.2
Charge
\pm 1
excitation state

After finding the ground states, we can also calculate the charge
\pm 1
excitation. The charge
\pm 1
excitation will be useful when we study the
z
-direction correlation induced by weak
z
-direction hopping via perturbation theory.

Since different layers are decoupled, we could treat each layer separately.
The charge
-1
excitation state
with in-plane momentum
\mathbf{k}^{xy}
, layer index
R_{z}
and spin
\sigma
can be described by

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.218)

We now show that the state in Eq.
S14.218
is an eigenstate of the interaction Hamiltonian by proving

\displaystyle[(H_{0}+H_{U}),\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}]|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=E^{hole}_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}),\sigma}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.219)

with
E^{hole}_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}
the corresponding energy of the excitation state.

We first calculate

\displaystyle[H_{U}+H_{0},\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma}]

(S14.220)

We note that

\displaystyle[O^{\dagger}_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})},\gamma_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\alpha,\sigma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma},\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\alpha}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})(-\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}})

(S14.221)

and then

\displaystyle[O_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})}O^{\dagger}_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})},\gamma_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{N_{xy}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}v_{\gamma n}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{k}_{xy})

\displaystyle[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma},(-\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{N_{xy}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}v_{\gamma n}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}}

(S14.222)

Then we have

\displaystyle[H_{U}+H_{0},\gamma_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},n}\frac{u_{n}}{2N_{xy}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}v_{\gamma n}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}}^{hole}\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}

(S14.223)

where

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{p}^{xy}}^{hole}=\sum_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},n}\frac{u_{n}}{2N_{xy}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}v_{\gamma n}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}-\mathbf{q}^{xy})

(S14.224)

Therefore, we have proved that

\displaystyle[(H_{0}+H_{U}),\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}]|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=E^{hole}_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.225)

This indicates, as long as,

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime}}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle\neq 0

(S14.226)

\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime}}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle
describes a charge
-1
excitation state with energy
E^{hole}_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}
.

We now aim to find the situation where Eq.
S14.226
holds. We evaluate the following equations explicitly

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime}}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.227)

We first note the following commutation relations

\displaystyle[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger},S^{-}_{R_{z}}]=-\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}^{\dagger},\quad[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow},S^{-}_{R_{z}}]=0

\displaystyle[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}^{\dagger},S^{-}_{R_{z}}]=0,\quad[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow},S^{-}_{R_{z}}]=\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}

(S14.228)

and

\displaystyle[(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{n},H]=0

\displaystyle[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger},(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{n}]=-n(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{n-1}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}^{\dagger},\quad[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow},(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{n}]=0

\displaystyle[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}^{\dagger},(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{n}]=0,\quad[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow},(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{n}]=n(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{n-1}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}

(S14.229)

Combining Eq.
S14.216
and Eq.
S14.229
, we find

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S}\bigg)\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S}\bigg)(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),S}\bigg)\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),S}\bigg)(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle+\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}(S-m(R_{z}))\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),S}\bigg)(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}(S-m(R_{z}))(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

(S14.230)

Since

\displaystyle|R_{z},S\rangle=\prod_{\mathbf{k}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

(S14.231)

then

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle\neq 0

(S14.232)

We thus conclude the following states give the charge
-1
excitation state

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle\neq 0,\quad\text{with }m(R_{z})\neq-S

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle\neq 0,\quad\text{with }m(R_{z})\neq S

(S14.233)

We next discuss the charge
+1
excitation. Similarly, we show that the following state describes the charge
+1
excitation

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.234)

by proving that

\displaystyle[(H_{0}+H_{U}),\gamma^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}]|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=E^{ele}_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.235)

with
E^{ele}_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}
the energy of the excitation state.

We now calculate

\displaystyle[H_{U}+H_{0},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma}]

(S14.236)

We first show

\displaystyle[O^{\dagger}_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\alpha,\sigma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{k})[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma},\gamma^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{xy}}}\sum_{\alpha}v_{\alpha n}^{*}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}}

(S14.237)

and then

\displaystyle[O_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})}O^{\dagger}_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{N_{xy}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}v_{\gamma n}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{k}_{xy})

\displaystyle[\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma},\gamma^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{N_{xy}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma}v_{\alpha n}^{*}v_{\gamma n}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{xy})\gamma_{(\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}),\sigma^{\prime\prime}}^{\dagger}

(S14.238)

Then we have

\displaystyle[H_{U}+H_{0},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}]|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=E_{\mathbf{p}^{xy}}^{ele}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.239)

where the energy function is

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{p}^{xy}}^{ele}=\sum_{\mathbf{q}^{xy},n}\frac{u_{n}}{2N_{xy}}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma,n}v_{\alpha n}^{*}v_{\gamma n}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{p}^{xy}+\mathbf{q}^{xy})V_{\gamma}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{xy}).

(S14.240)

Therefore, we prove
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle
describes a charge
+1
excitation state.

We note that a valid electron excitation needs to satisfy

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle\neq 0

(S14.241)

We now find the condition in which Eq.
S14.241
holds by evaluating

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle

(S14.242)

Using the commutation relation in Eq.
S14.229
and the definition of
|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle
in Eq.
S14.216
, we find

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}(S-m(R_{z}))(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S}\bigg)(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle

(S14.243)

Since

\displaystyle|R_{z},S\rangle=\prod_{\mathbf{k}}\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

(S14.244)

then

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle\neq 0

(S14.245)

We thus conclude the following states give the charge
+1
excitation state

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle\neq 0,\quad\text{with }m(R_{z})\neq S

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle\neq 0,\quad\text{with }m(R_{z})\neq-S

(S14.246)

From Eq.
S14.230
and Eq.
S14.243
, we can introduce the following charge
\pm 1
excitation state

\displaystyle|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle=(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle,\quad m(R_{z})\in\{-S,-S+1,...,S-1\}

\displaystyle|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle=(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle,\quad m(R_{z})\in\{-S+1,...,S-1,S\}

(S14.247)

with

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\uparrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S}\bigg)|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle

\displaystyle\gamma_{(\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}),\downarrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}(S-m(R_{z}))|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}(S-m(R_{z}))|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle=\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\bigg(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S}\bigg)|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle

(S14.248)

where
\alpha_{S,m}
has been introduced in Eq.
S14.216
.

We also comment that
|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h/e\rangle
is not normalized state.
We now calculate the norm of
|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle

\displaystyle\langle m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle=\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}(2S^{z}_{R_{z}})(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}S_{R_{z}}^{-}S_{R_{z}}^{+}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}(2S^{z}_{R_{z}})(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})-2}(2S^{z}_{R_{z}})S_{R_{z}}^{+}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})-2}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{2}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{S-m(R_{z})}\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})-m}2(S_{R_{z}}^{z})(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{m-1}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}S_{R_{z}}^{-}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{S-m(R_{z})}(2m(R_{z})+2m-1)\langle m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2})\langle m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle

(S14.249)

We note that

\displaystyle\langle S,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h|S,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle=\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle=1

(S14.250)

Thus, we define the norm as

\displaystyle\beta_{m(R_{z})}=\langle m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle=\prod_{m=m(R_{z})}^{S-1}(S^{2}-m^{2}),\quad m(R_{z})<S

\displaystyle\beta_{S}=1

(S14.251)

We then calculate the norm of
|M,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle

\displaystyle\langle m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle=\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{S-m(R_{z})}\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})-m}2(S_{R_{z}}^{z})(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{m-1}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}S_{R_{z}}^{-}(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{S-m(R_{z})}(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{S-m(R_{z})}(2m(R_{z})+2m-1)\langle m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2})\langle m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle

(S14.252)

Since

\displaystyle\langle S,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e|S,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle=\langle R_{z},S|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle=1

(S14.253)

we have

\displaystyle\langle m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle=\beta_{m(R_{z})}

(S14.254)

For future reference, we also note that by acting
S_{R_{z}}^{+}
, we could generate
|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e/h\rangle
state from
|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e/h\rangle
state. To observe this, we show

\displaystyle S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle=S^{+}_{R_{z}}(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(2S_{R_{z}}^{z}(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}+(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{1}S^{+}_{R_{z}}(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\bigg)\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{S-m(R_{z})}2(m(R_{z})+m-\frac{1}{2})(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle+(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}S_{R_{z}}^{+}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2})|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle

(S14.255)

Similarly,

\displaystyle S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle=S^{+}_{R_{z}}(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{S-m(R_{z})}2(m(R_{z})+m-\frac{1}{2})(S^{-}_{R_{z}})^{S-m(R_{z})-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle+(S_{R_{z}}^{-})^{S-m(R_{z})}S_{R_{z}}^{+}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}|R_{z},S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2})|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle

(S14.256)

In summary, we have

\displaystyle|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle=\frac{1}{S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},h\rangle

\displaystyle|m(R_{z})+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle=\frac{1}{S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},e\rangle

(S14.257)

XIV.2
z
direction hopping and out-of-plane antiferromagnetism

In the previous section, we discuss the 2D flat bands and the resulting in-plane ferromagnetic state. In the real system, even though the flat band is atomic along
z
direction, there could still be a weak hopping along
z
direction which will then generate out-of-plane antiferromagnetism.

We now introduce a weak
z
-direction hopping to the flat bands, such that different layers of the system are no longer decoupled. The additional
z
direction hopping we introduced (written in the original basis
d
) is

\displaystyle H_{z}=\sum_{\langle\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\rangle_{z},\sigma}t_{z,\alpha\gamma}d_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}d_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\gamma,\sigma}=\sum_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}t_{z,\alpha\gamma}\cos(k_z)d_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}^{\dagger}d_{\mathbf{k},\gamma,\sigma}

(S14.258)

where
\langle\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\rangle_{z}
denotes nearest neighbors along
z
directions and
t_{z,\alpha\gamma}
is the hopping strength.

Transforming to band basis and projecting to the flat bands, we have

\displaystyle H_{z,\gamma}=

\displaystyle PH_{z}P

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\bigg[\sum_{\alpha\gamma}t_{z,\alpha\gamma}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{xy})\bigg]\cos(k_z)\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{k}}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\cos(k_z)\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}

(S14.259)

where we utilize the fact that the wavefunction of flat bands
V_{\alpha}^{*}
only depends on
\mathbf{k}^{xy}
and introduce

\displaystyle f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}=\sum_{\alpha\gamma}t_{z,\alpha\gamma}V_{\alpha}^{*}(\mathbf{k}^{xy})V_{\gamma}(\mathbf{k}^{xy})

(S14.260)

We next treat the model with Schrieffer-Wolff transformation. We take

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle h_{0}+h_{1}

\displaystyle h_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{R_{z}}H_{R_{z}}=\sum_{R_{z}}\sum_{n,\mathbf{q}^{xy}}\frac{u_{n}}{2}O_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})}O_{n,(\mathbf{q}^{xy},R_{z})}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle h_{1}=

\displaystyle h_{HL}+h_{LH}

\displaystyle h_{HL}=

\displaystyle P_{H}\bigg[\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\langle R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}\rangle}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma}\bigg]P_{L}

\displaystyle h_{LH}=

\displaystyle P_{L}\bigg[\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\langle R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}\rangle}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma}\bigg]P_{H}

(S14.261)

with
h_{0}
is the unperturbed Hamiltonian and
h_{1}
is the perturbation (Eq.
S14.259
). We have also separated the perturbation term into two parts:
h_{HL}
and
h_{LH}
(similarly as Eq.
S13.172
). The projection operators are defined as

\displaystyle P_{L}=\sum_{\{m\}}|m\rangle\langle m|

\displaystyle P_{H}=\mathbb{I}-P_{L}

(S14.262)

where
P_{L}
is the projection operator of the low-energy space formed by the ground states
|m\rangle
of
h_{0}
(Eq.
S14.217
) and
P_{H}
is the projection operator of the high-energy states (excitation states of
h_{0}
, Eq.
S14.248
). Via Schrieffer-Wolff transformation, we will show that, the additional hopping along
z
direction (
h_{1}
) lifts the degeneracy of the ground states (Eq.
S14.217
) of the original Hamiltonian
h_{0}
.

In practice, we note that the
z
-direction hopping
h_{1}
could map a low-energy state to a high-energy state by creating an electron excitation at layer
R_{z}
and a hole excitation at layer
R_{z}^{\prime}
.
h_{1}
could also map a high-energy state to a low-energy state by reversing this procedure. To observe the effect of
h_{1}
, we consider each term of
h_{1}
separately

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma}=f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma}

\displaystyle h_{1}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},\langle R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}\rangle}h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime},\sigma}

(S14.263)

We mention that, for each layer,
h_{1}
effectively created a charge
\pm 1
excitation. More specifically,
acting
h_{1}
on the ground state will create a charge
-1
state at one layer and a charge
+1
state at another layer.
We comment that
h_{1}
will not create a Goldstone excitation. A Goldstone excitation for the in-plane ferromagnetic state requires the particle-hole excitation at the same layer which takes the form of
\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\sigma^{\prime}}
. Such types of excitation will not be created by simply acting
h_{1}
on the ground states.

Since we focus on the case of nearest-neighbor hopping along
z
direction, we have
R_{z}=R_{z}^{\prime}\pm 1
. We first consider the case with
R_{z}=R_{z}^{\prime}+1
. By acting
h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1,\sigma}
on the ground state and using Eq.
S14.248
, we could obtain

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1,\uparrow}|m\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg[f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}+1,\uparrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle|R_{z}+1,m(R_{z}+1)\rangle\bigg]\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+2}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},M(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg[-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}+1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}+1),-S})(S-m(R_{z}))\bigg]

\displaystyle\bigg(|m(R_{z})+1,R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle|m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+2}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},M(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle|\Phi_{a}\rangle

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1,\downarrow}|m\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg[f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z}+1,\downarrow}|R_{z},m(R_{z})\rangle|R_{z}+1,m(R_{z}+1)\rangle\bigg]\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+2}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg[\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}+1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(S-m(R_{z}+1))(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S)})\bigg]

\displaystyle\bigg(|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle|m(R_{z}+1)+1,R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+2}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle|\Phi_{a}\rangle

(S14.264)

where the additional
(-1)^{N_{xy}}
factor comes from the fermion anticommutation relation. Similarly, for the case of
R_{z}=R_{z}^{\prime}-1

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1,\uparrow}|m\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg[-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}-1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}+1}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}^{\prime}),-S})(S-m(R_{z}))\bigg]

\displaystyle\bigg(|m(R_{z}-1),R_{z}-1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle|m(R_{z})+1,R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+1}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},M(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle|\Phi_{a}\rangle

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1,\downarrow}|m\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-2}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},M(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg[\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}-1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}+1}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(S-m(R_{z}-1))(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S)})\bigg]

\displaystyle\bigg(|m(R_{z}-1)+1,R_{z}-1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+1}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle|\Phi_{a}\rangle

(S14.265)

where the additional
(-1)^{N_{xy}+1}
factor comes from the fermion anticommutation relation.
We can rewrite Eq.
S14.264
and Eq.
S14.264
in a simpler formula by introducing

\displaystyle|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle=\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg(|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle|m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+2}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle=\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-2}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg(|m(R_{z}-1),R_{z}-1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+1}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle

(S14.266)

where
|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}\rangle
denotes the states obtained by creating a charge
+1
excitation with momentum
\mathbf{k}^{xy}
at layer
R_{z}
and a charge
-1
excitation with momentum
\mathbf{k}^{xy}
at layer
R_{z}^{\prime}
on top of the ground state
|m\rangle
.

Then we have

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1,\uparrow}|m\rangle=

\displaystyle-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}+1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}+1),-S})(S-m(R_{z}))

\displaystyle\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1,\downarrow}|m\rangle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}+1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(S-m(R_{z}+1))(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S)})

\displaystyle\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}+1}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z}+1)^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1,\uparrow}|m\rangle=

\displaystyle-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}-1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}+1}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}-1),-S})(S-m(R_{z}))

\displaystyle\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1,\downarrow}|m\rangle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}-1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}+1}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(S-m(R_{z}-1))(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S})

\displaystyle\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}-1}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z}-1)^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle

(S14.267)

where we have used the relation given in Eq.
S14.257
.

We then use the projection operator defined in Eq.
S14.262
and find (from Eq.
S14.263
and Eq.
S14.267
)

\displaystyle P_{H}h_{1}P_{L}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},M,R_{z}}\bigg\{-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}+1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}+1),-S})(S-M(R_{z}))\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle\langle m|

\displaystyle+\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}+1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(S-m(R_{z}+1))(1-\delta_{M(R_{z}),-S)})\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}+1}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z}+1)^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle\langle m|

\displaystyle-\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}-1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}+1}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}-1),-S})(S-m(R_{z}))\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle\langle m|

\displaystyle+\alpha_{S,m(R_{z})}\alpha_{S,m(R_{z}-1)}(-1)^{N_{xy}+1}f_{z,\mathbf{k}^{xy}}(S-m(R_{z}-1))(1-\delta_{m(R_{z}),-S})\frac{S^{+}_{R_{z}-1}}{S^{2}-m(R_{z}-1)^{2}}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle\langle m|\bigg\}

(S14.268)

We can also find
P_{H}h_{1}P_{L}
via Hermitian conjugation

\displaystyle P_{L}h_{1}P_{H}=(P_{H}h_{1}P_{L})^{\dagger}

(S14.269)

Therefore, we have rewritten the perturbation term in a similar form as Eq.
S13.171

\displaystyle P_{H}h_{1}P_{L}+P_{L}h_{1}P_{H}=\sum_{ij}V_{ij}|L,i\rangle\langle H,j|+\text{h.c.}

(S14.270)

where
|L,i\rangle
denotes the ground states of
h_{0}
, and
|H,j\rangle
denotes the excitation state

\displaystyle|L,i\rangle\quad\sim\quad|m\rangle

\displaystyle|H,j\rangle\sim\quad S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}\pm 1\rangle,\quad S_{R_{z}\pm 1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}\pm 1\rangle

(S14.271)

The coefficient
V_{ij}
in Eq.
S14.270
can be obtained from Eq.
S14.268
.

To perform the Schrieffer-Wolff transformation, we also need to obtain the energy of excitation state
|H,j\rangle
. We find

\displaystyle h_{0}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg(H_{R_{z}}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle|m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle

\displaystyle+S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle H_{R_{z}+1}|m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+2}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{hole}+E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{ele})S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle

\displaystyle h_{0}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-2}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg(H_{R_{z}-1}|m(R_{z}-1),R_{z}-1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle

\displaystyle+|m(R_{z}-1),R_{z}-1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle H_{R_{z}}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+1}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\sum_{R_{z}}H_{R_{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{hole}+E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{ele})S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle

\displaystyle h_{0}S_{R_{z}+1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-1}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg(H_{R_{z}}|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle S_{R_{z}+1}^{+}|m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle

\displaystyle+|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle H_{R_{z}+1}S_{R_{z}+1}^{+}|m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+2}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{hole}+E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{ele})S_{R_{z}+1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle

\displaystyle h_{0}S_{R_{z}-1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=1}^{R_{z}-2}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\bigg(H_{R_{z}-1}S_{R_{z}-1}^{+}|m(R_{z}-1),R_{z}-1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle

\displaystyle+S_{R_{z}-1}^{+}|m(R_{z}-1),R_{z}-1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle H_{R_{z}}|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle\bigg)\bigotimes_{R_{z}^{\prime\prime}=R_{z}+1}^{L_{z}}|R_{z}^{\prime\prime},m(R_{z}^{\prime\prime})\rangle\sum_{R_{z}}H_{R_{z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{hole}+E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{ele})S_{R_{z}-1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle

where the charge
\pm 1
exctiation energy is introduced in Eq.
S14.224
and Eq.
S14.240
.

In summary, in the current problems, the high-energy states created by acting
h_{1}
on the ground states of
h_{0}
are

\displaystyle S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle,\quad S_{R_{z}+1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle

\displaystyle S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle,\quad S_{R_{z}-1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle

(S14.273)

with the excitation energies being

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{exct}

(S14.274)

for all four types of high-energy states, where we have also defined

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{exct}=E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{hole}+E_{\mathbf{k}^{xy}}^{ele}

(S14.275)

We also mention that, in Eq.
S14.273
, we utilize the definition of Eq.
S14.247
, such that all the excitation state can be obtained by acting
S^{+}_{R_{z}},S_{R_{z}\pm 1}^{+}
on the
|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}\pm 1\rangle
. In Eq.
S14.247
, we use
\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}
operators and
\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}^{\dagger}
operators to define the charge
\pm 1
excitation state.
We could also adapt other definitions (for example, we could use
\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\downarrow}
and
\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},\uparrow}^{\dagger}
operators), such that all the excitation states can be written in different formats.

However, we comment that the high-energy states (Eq.
S14.273
) are not normalized. We can evaluate its norm by using (Eq.
S14.257
, Eq.
S14.251
and Eq.
S14.254
)

\displaystyle\langle m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1|(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{\dagger}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e|S_{R_{z}}^{-}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m(R_{z}),R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle\langle m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h|m(R_{z}+1),R_{z}+1,\mathbf{k}^{xy},h\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}]^{2}\langle m(R_{z})+1,R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e|m(R_{z})+1,R_{z},\mathbf{k}^{xy},e\rangle\beta_{m(R_{z}+1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}]^{2}\beta_{m(R_{z})+1}\beta_{m(R_{z}+1)}=\eta_{m(R_{z}),m(R_{z}+1)}

\displaystyle\langle m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1|(S_{R_{z}+1}^{+})^{\dagger}S_{R_{z}+1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}+1\rangle=[S^{2}-m(R_{z}+1)^{2}]\beta_{m(R_{z}+1)+1}\beta_{m(R_{z})}=\eta_{m(R_{z}+1),m(R_{z})}

\displaystyle\langle m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1|(S_{R_{z}}^{+})^{\dagger}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle=[S^{2}-m(R_{z})^{2}]\beta_{m(R_{z})+1}\beta_{m(R_{z}-1)}=\eta_{m(R_{z}),m(R_{z}-1)}

\displaystyle\langle m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1|(S_{R_{z}-1}^{+})^{\dagger}S_{R_{z}-1}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}-1\rangle=[S^{2}-m(R_{z}-1)^{2}]\beta_{m(R_{z}-1)+1}\beta_{m(R_{z})}=\eta_{m(R_{z}-1),m(R_{z})}

(S14.276)

where we have introduced the normalization factor

\displaystyle\eta_{M,M^{\prime}}=[S^{2}-M^{2}]^{2}\beta_{M+1}\beta_{M^{\prime}}

(S14.277)

Now we can perform Schrieffer-Wolff transformation (Eq.
S13.179
)

\displaystyle H_{SW}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j,m}V_{im}V_{jm}^{*}\left(\frac{1}{E_{L,i}-E_{H,m}}+\frac{1}{E_{L,j}-E_{H,m}}\right)|L,i\rangle\langle L,j|

(S14.278)

We first comment that the Schrieffer-Wolff transformation describes the virtual procedure from low-energy state
|L,i\rangle
to high-energy state
|H,m\rangle
and then back to low-energy state
|L,j\rangle
. Therefore, the following virtual procedures could be generated and contribute to the
H_{SW}

\displaystyle|L,j\rangle\rightarrow|H,m\rangle\rightarrow|L,i\rangle

\displaystyle I:

\displaystyle|m\rangle\rightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{\eta_{m(R_{z}),m(R_{z}^{\prime})}}}S_{R_{z}}^{+}|m,\mathbf{k}^{xy},R_{z},R_{z}^{\prime}\rangle\rightarrow|m\rangle

\displaystyle II:
